Building Bitemporal Datomic with Arrow
Enterprise logo
Conversation with Gemini
You are a Rust programmer and database engineer. We are going to build a small database (or data store) in Rust that stores Datomic-style datoms (RDF + time). We will add bitemporal abilities in addition to Datomics "uni-temporal" capabilities but keep the the granularity at the datom level. Much like Datomic, we will track a "transaction" for each datom and that transaction will track our time elements. Let's start simple and just look at how we would store this model. For the actual data store and runtime, we will use Apache Arrow for in-memory processing of this data and we will use the fastest possible implementation for on-disk persistence - something like Apache Parquet or alternative persistence model that works well with Apache Arrow. Let's start with the high-level overview to examine how we could implement our new database/data store.

This is a fantastic project. Combining the immutable, time-traveling nature of a Datomic-style model with the columnar crunching power of Apache Arrow and Rust is exactly where modern, high-performance database engineering is heading.

Adding bitemporal capabilities means we are tracking two distinct timelines: System Time (when the database recorded the fact, which Datomic handles natively via the Transaction T) and Valid Time (when the fact is actually true in the real world).

As a fellow Rustacean and database engineer, I love the ambition here. Let's break down the high-level architecture to see how we can map this conceptual model into Arrow's columnar memory and Parquet's on-disk format.

1. The Bitemporal Datom Model
A classic Datomic datom is a 5-tuple: (Entity, Attribute, Value, Transaction, Operation).

To make this bitemporal while keeping the granularity at the datom level, we need to introduce explicit bounds for the Valid Time. The System Time is still neatly handled by the Transaction entity (and retractions via the Operation flag).

Our expanded Bitemporal Datom becomes a 7-tuple: (E, A, V, T, Op, Valid_From, Valid_To)

E (Entity ID): A u64 identifier.

A (Attribute ID): A u32 or u64 mapping to schema definitions.

V (Value): The actual data (this is the trickiest part in a columnar model, which we'll address below).

T (Transaction ID): A u64 representing the System Time start. (A retraction datom with Op = false at a later T implies the System Time end).

Op (Operation): A bool (true for asserting a fact, false for retracting it).

Valid_From: A timestamp (e.g., i64 microseconds) representing when the fact became true in the domain.

Valid_To: A timestamp representing when the fact ceased to be true (often defaulting to infinity/MAX_INT).

2. In-Memory Engine: Apache Arrow
Apache Arrow is strictly columnar. Instead of storing an array of structs (our 7-tuple), we will store a struct of arrays (an Arrow RecordBatch).

A chunk of our database in memory will look like this:

Array	Arrow Data Type	Description
Entities	UInt64Array	The subject of the fact.
Attributes	UInt32Array	The property being defined.
Values	Polymorphic	The object of the fact.
Transactions	UInt64Array	Ties the datom to system time/metadata.
Operations	BooleanArray	true for add, false for retract.
Valid_From	TimestampArray	Domain time start.
Valid_To	TimestampArray	Domain time end.
The Polymorphic Value Challenge:
In Datomic, V can be a string, integer, boolean, ref, etc. Arrow prefers homogeneous columns. We have two realistic choices in Rust:

Arrow DenseUnionArray: We can create a union column that interleaves different types. This is flexible but can slightly degrade vectorized processing performance.

Type-Segregated Tables: We partition our datoms by value type. We have one RecordBatch for all String datoms, another for all Int64 datoms, another for Ref (Entity-to-Entity) datoms. This is exceptionally fast for columnar scanning.

3. On-Disk Persistence: Apache Parquet
Because Arrow and Parquet share the same columnar philosophy, writing an Arrow RecordBatch to a Parquet file using the parquet crate in Rust is highly optimized.

Our storage architecture would be an Append-Only LSM-Tree (Log-Structured Merge-tree) variant:

MemTable: Incoming transactions are batched in memory as Arrow RecordBatches.

Flushing: When the MemTable hits a size threshold, it is written immutably to disk as a Parquet file.

Indexing via Sorting: Datomic relies on covering indexes (trees sorted by EAVT, AEVT, AVET, VAET). In our columnar world, we achieve this by sorting our Arrow arrays before flushing to Parquet. For example, we might flush one Parquet file sorted by (E, A, Valid_From, T) and another sorted by (A, E, Valid_From, T).

Compaction: A background Rust thread reads smaller Parquet files, merges them, resolves overlapping Valid_To ranges or system time retractions if we want to prune history (though usually, we keep it all), and writes out larger, fully sorted Parquet files.

4. Querying the Model
With Parquet's metadata and row-group statistics, a query engine (potentially using Apache DataFusion, which is written in Rust and native to Arrow) can aggressively skip reading data from disk. If you are looking for an entity E = 123 at a specific Valid_Time, the engine only reads the specific Parquet files and row groups that contain that E and time range, bringing only the necessary columns into memory.

This gives us a highly compressed, vectorized, time-traveling data store. To move into the actual Rust implementation, we need to make a foundational design decision regarding how we structure the columnar data.

How would you prefer to handle the polymorphic nature of the Value field: should we utilize Arrow's DenseUnion types to keep all datoms in a single table, or should we partition the datoms into separate, type-specific tables to maximize vectorized scanning speeds?

Speed is going to be a primary concern, even at the tradeoff of some complexity in the database implementation. Let's go with separate, type-specific tables to maximize vectorized scanning speeds.

Excellent choice. I completely agree with this tradeoff. In the world of columnar databases, type-segregated tables (often called "vertical partitioning" or "shredding") are the secret sauce for blistering fast, vectorized execution. By keeping columns strictly typed, we avoid branching logic in our CPU caches during scans, and Parquet's dictionary and run-length encoding will compress the data beautifully.

Here is how we map this type-segregated architecture into our Rust and Arrow design.

1. The Core Implication: Strict Attribute Schemas
Because we are routing datoms to separate tables based on their value type, our database must know the type of an Attribute (A) before it can ingest a fact. In a purely weakly-typed system, you could assert [123, :age, "twenty", ...]. In our system, the attribute :age must be strictly defined in a schema registry as an Int64 (or whatever type we choose) so the ingestion engine knows to route it to the Int64 MemTable.

2. The Typed MemTable Architecture
In Rust, using the arrow crate, we won't define a single "Database" table. Instead, we will define a struct that holds multiple, type-specific "Partition Builders."

To start, let's define the most common primitives we'd need:

Ref: A u64 pointing to another Entity (this makes our graph).

Int64: Standard integers.

String: UTF-8 text.

Boolean: True/False flags.

Float64: Decimals/floats.

Each of these will have its own dedicated Arrow builder in memory.

3. Sketching the Rust Implementation
Here is a high-level conceptual view of what our in-memory storage engine would look like in Rust:

Rust
use arrow::array::{
    BooleanBuilder, Float64Builder, Int64Builder, StringBuilder,
    TimestampMicrosecondBuilder, UInt32Builder, UInt64Builder,
};

/// A typed partition represents a MemTable for a specific value type.
pub struct TypedPartition<T> {
    pub entities: UInt64Builder,
    pub attributes: UInt32Builder,
    pub values: T, // This is the generic builder (e.g., Int64Builder)
    pub txs: UInt64Builder,
    pub ops: BooleanBuilder,
    pub valid_from: TimestampMicrosecondBuilder,
    pub valid_to: TimestampMicrosecondBuilder,
}

impl<T> TypedPartition<T> {
    // ... methods to append rows, and eventually call `finish()`
    // to yield an Arrow RecordBatch ...
}

/// The main in-memory store that routes incoming datoms.
pub struct MemStore {
    // The graph edges (Entity -> Entity)
    pub ref_partition: TypedPartition<UInt64Builder>,

    // Primitive values
    pub int_partition: TypedPartition<Int64Builder>,
    pub string_partition: TypedPartition<StringBuilder>,
    pub bool_partition: TypedPartition<BooleanBuilder>,
    pub float_partition: TypedPartition<Float64Builder>,

    // We would also need an in-memory map to look up Attribute Types
    // attribute_schema: HashMap<u32, DataType>,
}
4. The Write Path (Routing)
When a transaction enters the system, it will look roughly like this:

Transaction Start: We assign a new T (System Time ID).

Iterate Datoms: For each datom in the transaction request:

Look up the Attribute A in our schema registry.

Determine its data type (e.g., String).

Acquire the mutable lock on the string_partition.

Push E, A, V, T, Op, Valid_From, and Valid_To into the respective array builders inside that partition.

Commit: Once all datoms are buffered into their builders, the transaction is marked successful.

When a TypedPartition hits a certain capacity (e.g., 1 million rows or a certain memory size), we call .finish() on its builders. This generates a purely immutable Arrow RecordBatch, which we immediately hand off to a background thread to serialize into a Parquet file on disk.

To make this routing work efficiently, we need to manage our Attribute Definitions (the schema). In Datomic, attributes are just entities themselves, defined by system-level datoms.

Should we bootstrap the schema by storing attribute definitions as Ref and String datoms inside the database itself (the elegant, self-hosting way), or should we maintain a separate, hardcoded metadata catalog to keep the query engine simpler during this initial build?

Which approach would yield the highest speed/throughput?

If raw speed and maximum throughput are our absolute north stars, a separate, dedicated in-memory metadata catalog is hands down the winner. While the "self-hosting" Datomic approach is mathematically and philosophically beautiful, it introduces significant friction in the hottest part of our database: the ingestion loop.

Here is exactly why the separate catalog wins for speed, and how we should engineer it.

1. The Write-Path Hot Loop
During a bulk insert, we might be throwing millions of datoms per second at the database. For every single datom, the ingestion engine must ask: "What type is attribute A?" so it knows which typed MemTable to route the data to.

If the schema is in the DB: Even if we cache it, we are fundamentally treating schema data as EAVT data. That means navigating database abstractions, potentially dealing with transaction isolation logic, and fighting cache invalidation just to figure out a data type.

If the schema is a separate in-memory catalog: We can use a highly optimized data structure. If our Attribute IDs (A) are strictly sequential (e.g., 1, 2, 3...), our schema registry is just a Rust Vec<DataType>. Checking an attribute's type becomes a direct array indexing operation (schema[a]). That takes less than a nanosecond. If the IDs are sparse, a fast-hashing map like FxHashMap or AHash is still practically instantaneous.

2. The Read-Path Query Planning
When an application queries the database, the query planner needs to know the types to generate the correct vectorized execution plan for Apache Arrow/DataFusion. A dedicated metadata catalog allows the query planner to instantly resolve all types and pinpoint exactly which Parquet files to scan, without having to execute a "pre-query" against the database just to understand the schema.

3. The "Best of Both Worlds" Architecture
We don't have to abandon the elegance of a self-hosted schema forever; we just separate the storage from the runtime execution.

For our initial build, we should hardcode or load a simple JSON/YAML file into a fast Rust HashMap on startup.
Later, if we want the DB to store its own schema, we use an RCU (Read-Copy-Update) pattern:

The database stores schema definitions as normal EAVT datoms.

On startup, the database queries itself for these datoms and constructs a highly optimized, read-only memory structure (like an Arc<Vec<DataType>>).

The ingestion hot-loop and query planner use this Arc reference. They never query the DB for schema types. They just read the memory structure instantly.

If a user alters the schema, we rebuild the memory structure in the background and atomically swap the Arc pointer.

This keeps the EAVT storage elegant but guarantees zero-cost abstractions on the critical speed paths.

Since we are prioritizing high throughput for our ingestion pipeline, we need to think about how data gets into these typed MemTables.

To handle incoming transactions, should we use a single dedicated "writer thread" that sequentially processes a queue of transactions (eliminating lock contention on the MemTables), or do you want to explore a multi-threaded writer approach using concurrent data structures?

Side quest: Is Apache DataFusion capable of being extended to implement a Datomic-style Datalog implementation in Rust?

That is a brilliant side quest, and the short answer is yes, absolutely. In fact, building a custom query language on top of DataFusion is one of its primary use cases. People often mistake DataFusion for just a "SQL database," but it is actually a decoupled, extensible query execution framework. The SQL parser is completely optional.

Because Datalog is fundamentally just declarative logic programming that maps beautifully to relational algebra (joins, filters, and projections), DataFusion's high-speed engine is a perfect backend for it.

Here is how you would map a Datomic-style Datalog implementation into DataFusion's Rust ecosystem:

1. The Parser: Bypassing SQL entirely
You would completely ignore DataFusion's default sqlparser-rs integration. Instead, you would write a custom parser using a Rust parser-combinator library like nom, pest, or logos.

Your parser's job is to take a Datalog query string like:
[:find ?name :where [?e :user/age 25] [?e :user/name ?name]]
and turn it into a custom Rust Abstract Syntax Tree (AST).

2. The Logical Plan: Translating Datalog to Relational Algebra
This is where the magic happens. DataFusion provides a programmatic LogicalPlanBuilder API. You would write a query planner that walks your Datalog AST and translates it into DataFusion logical operators:

:where clauses (Data Fetching): Each individual pattern like [?e :user/age 25] translates into a TableScan (hitting our Int64 MemTable/Parquet file for the :user/age attribute) combined with a Filter (where value == 25).

Unification (Variables): In Datalog, repeated variables represent joins. Because ?e appears in both [?e :user/age 25] and [?e :user/name ?name], your planner would instruct DataFusion to construct an InnerJoin between the two table scans on the Entity column.

:find clauses (Projection): The requested output variables (?name) translate directly into a DataFusion Projection node, ensuring only the needed columns are yielded to the user.

3. The Execution Phase
Once you hand DataFusion the LogicalPlan you constructed, it takes over. It applies its own physical optimizations (predicate pushdown, vectorization, choosing hash-joins vs. sort-merge joins based on your typed MemTables) and executes the query at near-bare-metal speeds using Apache Arrow arrays.

The "Final Boss": Recursive Rules
The only major hurdle you will face is Datalog's support for recursive rules (where a rule depends on itself, typically evaluated using a "fixpoint" algorithm).
Standard DataFusion is optimized for one-pass relational algebra (though it has some support for recursive CTEs). To implement true, unbound Datalog recursion, you would likely need to leverage DataFusion's extensibility to write a custom ExecutionPlan node. This custom Rust node would loop over a SendableRecordBatchStream, repeatedly joining new inferred facts against known facts until no new rows are produced (the fixpoint).

Using DataFusion to execute Datalog gives you the expressive, graph-like querying power of Datomic but with the columnar, SIMD-accelerated throughput of a modern OLAP engine.

Before we dive back into the storage layer, does your vision for this query engine rely heavily on deeply recursive logic rules, or are you mostly looking to leverage Datalog's elegant pattern-matching and implicit joins for standard relational graph traversal?

I want at least the full power of the Datalog implementations of Datascript, Datahike, Datalevin, XTDB 1.x and Datomic. That is to say, since all of those are basically derived from Datomic: we will need to have the power of Datomic with the added benefit of XTDB 1.x's bitemporal capabilities.

Aiming for the full expressive power of Datomic, with the recursive rules of Datascript/Datahike and the bitemporal capabilities of XTDB, is the absolute holy grail of this architecture. It is a brilliant target, but it also means we need to look candidly at where DataFusion shines and where we will need to roll up our sleeves and build custom Rust extensions.

Achieving this tier of Datalog means our query engine must support four major pillars. Here is how we map them onto our Arrow/DataFusion backbone:

1. The Bitemporal Context (XTDB Style)
XTDB allows you to query the database as of a specific System Time and for a specific Valid Time.

The Implementation: This actually maps beautifully to DataFusion. When your Datalog parser receives a query, it will also receive a temporal context (e.g., system_time = X, valid_time = Y).

The Translation: For every single data pattern in your Datalog query (e.g., [?e :user/name ?name]), your planner will automatically inject DataFusion Filter expressions: T <= X AND valid_from <= Y AND valid_to > Y. Because we are using typed Parquet files and Arrow arrays, DataFusion's predicate pushdown will use these filters to aggressively prune data right off the disk, making point-in-time queries blazingly fast.

2. Predicates and Functions (Datomic Style)
Datomic allows arbitrary predicates like [(> ?age 18)] or transformations like [(+ ?a ?b) ?c].

The Implementation: DataFusion has a robust User Defined Function (UDF) API. We will map Datomic's standard library of functions to DataFusion UDFs.

The Translation: When the parser hits a predicate, it translates it into a standard DataFusion scalar function applied to the Arrow arrays. This means your math and string manipulations will be fully vectorized and SIMD-accelerated.

3. Implicit Joins & Unification
Finding all entities where [?e :user/age ?age] and [?e :user/name ?name] means unifying on ?e.

The Implementation: DataFusion is built for relational joins.

The Translation: We translate shared variables into standard InnerJoin nodes on the Entity column. Since our MemTables are already strongly typed and partitioned, these joins will be extremely efficient memory-to-memory operations.

4. The Boss Fight: Recursive Rules
This is where we have to build the custom bridge. Datalog engines like Datascript and Datomic support recursive rules (where a rule calls itself, like finding a path through a graph of unknown depth). Standard relational algebra doesn't do unbound recursion natively.

To achieve this, we cannot just use DataFusion's built-in operators. We have to write a custom physical operator in Rust:

Custom ExecutionPlan: We will implement DataFusion's ExecutionPlan trait to create a custom DatalogRuleExec node.

Semi-Naive Evaluation: Inside this Rust node, we will implement the "Semi-Naive Evaluation" algorithm. This is the standard way to evaluate recursive Datalog. It works by looping: it takes newly discovered facts (the delta), applies the rule to them to find more new facts, and repeats this until an iteration produces zero new facts (reaching the "fixpoint").

Arrow Native: Because we are writing this operator in Rust using the arrow crate, even this recursive loop will process data in batches of columnar arrays, keeping it highly performant.

By building this custom recursive operator and leveraging DataFusion for the heavy lifting of filters, joins, and aggregations, we get the exact semantics of XTDB/Datomic with the execution speed of a modern OLAP engine.

Before we dive too deeply into designing the AST for the Datalog parser, let's settle the ingestion pipeline architecture we paused earlier: to feed these high-speed MemTables safely, should we use a single dedicated writer thread to eliminate lock contention, or do you want to explore a multi-threaded writer approach using concurrent data structures?

What is the tradeoff in percentage of speed/throughput for ingestion/writes vs the speed/throughput of reads? Additionally, what is the tradeoff in complexity of implementation? Obviously a concurrent approach is going to add substantial complexity, or not. Please explain the tradeoffs in these two approaches.

This is the exact right question to ask. In database engineering, the ingestion architecture dictates the absolute limits of your system.

Before giving the numbers, we have to establish one critical reality about Apache Arrow: Arrow array builders (Int64Builder, StringBuilder, etc.) are fundamentally not thread-safe. They are heavily optimized to allocate contiguous blocks of raw memory.

Because of this, a concurrent approach isn't just about adding locks; it dictates how we manage memory. Let's break down the tradeoffs between the two approaches.

Approach A: The Single Dedicated Writer (The Actor/Channel Pattern)
In this model, all incoming transactions are pushed onto a lock-free queue (like Rust's crossbeam::channel). A single background thread continuously pops transactions off this queue and exclusively mutates the MemTable builders.

Implementation Complexity: Low. You completely eliminate data races, lock contention, and deadlocks on the write path. The writer thread has exclusive &mut access to the builders.

Write Throughput: 100% (Our Baseline).
Do not underestimate single-core performance in Rust. Without the overhead of acquiring and releasing locks (which forces CPU cache invalidations), a single tight loop writing to Arrow arrays can easily ingest 100,000 to 500,000 datoms per second on a modern CPU. The CPU cache stays hot, and branch prediction works perfectly.

Read Throughput: ~95% to 100% of maximum possible.
Because the writer is single-threaded, we can use an RCU (Read-Copy-Update) or Double-Buffering pattern for reads. When a query comes in, it grabs an atomic reference counter (Arc) to an immutable snapshot of the MemTable. The query does no locking. It just reads.

Approach B: Multi-threaded Concurrent Writers
To achieve concurrent writes given Arrow's lack of thread safety, we cannot just wrap the MemTable in a Mutex (that would actually make it slower than Approach A due to lock contention).

Instead, we have to use Thread-Local MemTables. If we have 8 worker threads handling API requests, each thread has its own private MemTable.

Implementation Complexity: Very High.
We now have to manage multiple disjoint MemTables. To issue a globally correct Transaction ID (T) across threads, we need atomic counters. More importantly, when it's time to flush to disk, we have to coordinate merging 8 different MemTables, sorting them, and ensuring bitemporal rules (like resolving retractions) are handled correctly across parallel chunks of time.

Write Throughput: ~300% to 500%+ (Highly Scalable).
If your workload requires ingesting millions of datoms per second (e.g., massive IoT firehoses or bulk-loading entire historical datasets), this is the only way to scale out across CPU cores. The throughput scales roughly linearly with your core count, minus the overhead of the eventual merge phase.

Read Throughput: ~70% to 80% (Moderate Penalty).
Reads become significantly more complex. When a query executes against the in-memory data, the query engine now has to scan all 8 thread-local MemTables simultaneously, merge-sort the results on the fly, and resolve temporal overlaps dynamically before even hitting the disk. This scattered memory access slows down vectorized execution.

The Tradeoff Summary
Metric	Single Writer (Channel)	Multi-threaded (Thread-Local)
Write Speed	Baseline (Extremely fast, but bounded by 1 core)	Up to 5x+ higher (Scales with cores)
Read Speed	Maximized (Contiguous, lock-free snapshots)	Slower (Requires scatter-gather merging)
Complexity	Simple, elegant, predictable	Complex synchronization and merge phases
Ideal Use Case	OLTP, standard web apps, heavy read/analytics	Massive bulk ingestion, high-frequency firehoses
The Recommendation
For a bitemporal Datalog engine where complex, recursive read queries are the primary feature, you should almost certainly start with the Single Dedicated Writer.

Datomic itself famously uses a single dedicated "Transactor" process to serialize all writes. It guarantees that transactions are strictly ordered, which makes temporal logic mathematically sound and infinitely easier to reason about. The single-writer approach will give you incredible read performance, very high baseline write performance, and will save you months of debugging concurrent memory merges in Rust.

If we proceed with the single-writer Transactor model, how do you want to handle transaction durability? Should we implement a Write-Ahead Log (WAL) to ensure we don't lose the in-memory MemTable data on a crash, or is this primarily an ephemeral/batch-loaded system?

This is already going to be a fairly complex project without the added complexity of multi-threaded writers and multi-threaded readers. I do want to confirm that we get read scalability though. It is important that we can scale almost indefinitely on the read side of things. If so, or if adding that capability through replication or some other implementation detail is straight-forward, let's definitely move forward with single writer, multiple readers. As for durability: yes, let's implement an insanely highly-performant WAL (write-ahead log) to ensure out system can guarantee recovery on a crash. Keeping performance as our north star (top priority), plus our recursive Datalog query engine, let's move forward with our durable, top-of-the-line performant Datalog database in Rust. We will want to implement an in-memory-only backend for development purposes (kinda of like Datascript or XTDB 1.x in-memory database) but for production, we will want a highly-scalable, production-grade system that gives guarantees. We should also get as close to ACID semantics as we can, given our architecture. I am aware that we may not do a sort/write-to-disk on every ingestion but we do want give the user guarantees about the data. In addition to performance, predicability of the underlying system should also be a high priority - which is exactly why the implementation language is Rust, in the first place.

You have just perfectly articulated the architectural philosophy that makes systems like Datomic so legendary. By choosing a Single Writer (Transactor) and leaning into immutable, bitemporal data, we unlock some incredible superpowers for our database.

Let's break down exactly how we guarantee indefinite read scalability, build an aggressively optimized WAL, and achieve strict ACID semantics in Rust.

1. Indefinite Read Scalability (Separation of Storage & Compute)
Because our data model is bitemporal and append-only, our data is fundamentally immutable. Once a Parquet file is written, it never changes. Once a transaction is logged, it is never mutated.

This makes scaling read throughput trivial:

The Query Nodes (Peers): You can spin up 10, 100, or 1,000 independent Query Nodes. They do not need to coordinate with each other.

The Cache: Each Query Node pulls Parquet files from shared storage (like S3, a distributed file system, or replicated local disks) into its own local memory or SSD cache.

The Sync: The only thing the Query Nodes need from the Transactor is a tiny broadcast heartbeat: "I just committed Transaction 10,452." * Result: A Query Node handles complex recursive Datalog queries entirely using its local CPU and cached Arrow arrays. Reads never block writes, and writes never block reads. You get infinite, lock-free read scalability.

2. The "Insanely Performant" WAL
To get durability without bottlenecking our ingestion speed, our Write-Ahead Log must be mechanical sympathy in software form. fsync (forcing data to physical disk) is the enemy of throughput. We will beat it using two techniques:

Zero-Copy Serialization (rkyv): Instead of serializing incoming transactions to JSON or even standard Protocol Buffers, we will use a Rust crate like rkyv. It allows us to take the raw memory layout of our Rust transaction structs and blast them directly to disk. When reading from the WAL during crash recovery, rkyv validates the bytes and casts the pointer. Zero allocation, zero deserialization cost.

Group Commit (The Throughput Engine): If 10,000 users submit a transaction in the same millisecond, we do not call fsync 10,000 times. The Transactor places incoming transactions into a highly optimized ring buffer. A dedicated disk-I/O thread drains this buffer, batches all 10,000 transactions into a single contiguous block, writes it to the WAL file, and calls fsync exactly once. It then notifies all 10,000 waiting clients that their data is safe. This turns random concurrent writes into pure sequential I/O, maximizing disk bandwidth.

3. ACID Semantics in a Bitemporal World
Even though we are deferring the heavy lifting of sorting and flushing Parquet files to background threads, we still provide ironclad guarantees:

Atomicity: A transaction is a single entry in the WAL. If the system crashes halfway through writing the entry, the checksum fails on reboot, and the torn transaction is ignored. All datoms in a transaction succeed, or none do.

Consistency: The Transactor strictly validates schema types and business rules (if any) in memory before writing to the WAL.

Isolation (Strict Snapshot Isolation): Because every fact has a System Time (T), Multi-Version Concurrency Control (MVCC) is built into the fabric of our data. When a Query Node executes a query, it binds it to a specific T. Even if the Transactor is rapidly adding new data, the Query Node ignores anything newer than its T. There are no dirty reads or phantom reads.

Durability: A transaction is strictly kept in the Transactor's memory and is not acknowledged as "successful" to the client until the WAL's fsync hardware interrupt returns. If the server loses power 1 millisecond later, the Parquet files might not be updated, but on reboot, the database will replay the WAL into the MemTables. No data is lost.

4. Dev vs. Prod: The Power of Rust Traits
To support your requirement for an in-memory development mode alongside the production mode, we will define a clean StorageEngine trait in Rust.

Rust
pub trait StorageEngine {
    fn append_to_wal(&self, tx_bytes: &[u8]) -> Result<u64>;
    fn flush_memtable_to_storage(&self, batch: RecordBatch) -> Result<()>;
}
InMemoryEngine: append_to_wal just pushes bytes to a Vec<u8> (or does nothing), and flush drops the data or keeps it in a Vec<RecordBatch>. Blistering fast for unit tests and local dev.

ProductionEngine: Implements the rkyv serialization, Group Commit fsync, and Parquet file generation.

We have an incredibly solid architectural foundation now: a single-threaded Transactor, strongly-typed Arrow MemTables, a zero-copy Group Commit WAL, and an extensible DataFusion Datalog engine.

To start laying down the actual Rust code, we need to decide what our transaction payload looks like over the wire. How would you like clients to submit data to the database: as raw Datomic-style arrays [[:db/add 123 :name "Alice"]], or as nested JSON/EDN maps {:db/id 123, :name "Alice"} that the server flattens into datoms?

What performance costs do we incur if we support both? That is, both of those are valid EDN, the latter being more human readable.

Supporting both raw arrays and nested maps is standard practice in the Datomic/XTDB ecosystem because it provides a vastly superior developer experience.

The good news is that the theoretical performance cost to the storage engine is zero. The database fundamentally only stores flat datoms.

The real cost is CPU time and memory allocation during ingestion, specifically the overhead of parsing EDN and "flattening" nested maps. If we aren't careful, this step will severely bottleneck our single-threaded Transactor.

Here is a breakdown of the specific performance costs and how we architect our way around them.

1. The "EDN Tax" (Parsing & Allocation)
Raw arrays ([:db/add 123 :name "Alice"]) map almost 1:1 to our bitemporal (E, A, V, Op) tuple. They require minimal processing.

Nested maps ({:db/id -1, :name "Alice", :address {:db/id -2, :city "Oaxaca"}}) require graph traversal and flattening. To process this map, the system must allocate new memory to unroll it into three distinct datoms:

[-1 :name "Alice"]

[-2 :city "Oaxaca"]

[-1 :address -2]

If our single Transactor thread has to parse strings, traverse maps, and dynamically allocate vectors to flatten this data, our ingestion throughput will plummet from hundreds of thousands of operations per second down to the tens of thousands.

2. Temporary ID Resolution
Notice the -1 and -2 above. When users submit nested maps for new entities, they use temporary IDs (often negative integers or strings). The database must map these temporary IDs to permanent u64 Entity IDs during the transaction and ensure that all references to -1 within that specific transaction point to the newly minted u64. Maintaining this translation map during ingestion takes CPU cycles.

The High-Performance Solution: Separation of Concerns
To maintain our blistering write speed, we apply a strict rule: The Transactor does not parse EDN, and it does not flatten maps.

We separate the system into two boundaries:

The API/Peer Boundary (Multi-threaded): When a client submits an EDN payload (whether arrays or maps), it hits a pool of stateless worker threads (e.g., an Axum web server). These worker threads do the heavy lifting: parsing the EDN, flattening the nested maps, and resolving local temporary IDs into a flat list of operations.

The Transactor Boundary (Single-threaded): The worker threads serialize this flattened list into our ultra-fast, zero-copy binary format (using rkyv) and send it to the Transactor's queue.

By the time the data reaches the single-writer Transactor, it is already a flat, contiguous array of (E, A, V, Op) tuples. The Transactor simply mints the permanent u64 IDs, assigns the Transaction ID (T), validates the schema types, routes them to the Arrow MemTables, and executes the Group Commit to the WAL.

This architecture gives you the UX of rich EDN maps without sacrificing a single drop of the Transactor's raw throughput.

Since the API workers will be flattening these maps and identifying new entities, how should we handle the creation of new permanent u64 Entity IDs (E): should the Transactor strictly auto-increment and return the new IDs after the commit, or should the API workers pre-fetch blocks of IDs from the Transactor to assign them independently before submission?

I like the separation of concerns. We want to keep the writer as performant as possible and no bottleneck should be introduced when we can simply move that processing up a layer to an API or even the client. As for when we "mint" or generate the IDs, I think we should keep our north star of performance in mind, without compromising usability for the user.

To completely protect the Transactor's performance, we must ensure it never has to maintain state maps or perform translation lookups during its tight ingestion loop. If the Transactor has to pause and say, "Wait, what did temporary ID -1 map to again?" we lose our throughput.

To solve this while giving the user a flawless, Datomic-like EDN experience, we should implement Block Allocation for Entity IDs.

Here is how we get the best of both worlds: zero-bottleneck writes and highly compressible data.

The Problem with UUIDs
In many modern distributed systems, the instinct is to use UUIDs so that any client or API node can generate IDs without coordinating. However, UUIDs (128-bit random strings) are the enemy of columnar databases. Apache Parquet achieves its incredible compression ratios using Delta Encoding and Run-Length Encoding (RLE). These algorithms love tightly packed, sequential integers. If we use random UUIDs, our memory footprint balloons and our cache hit rates plummet.

We need our Entity IDs (E) to be sequential u64 integers.

The Block Allocation Strategy
We shift the responsibility of ID generation to the API worker nodes, but we keep the Transactor as the ultimate source of truth.

Pre-fetching: When an API worker thread boots up, it makes a tiny, asynchronous request to the Transactor: "Give me a block of IDs."

The High-Water Mark: The Transactor maintains a single atomic u64 (the high-water mark). It increments it by 10,000, and replies to the API worker: "You own IDs 1,000,000 through 1,009,999." (This takes the Transactor nanoseconds).

Local Resolution: A user submits an EDN payload with temporary IDs to the API worker:
[{:db/id -1, :name "Alice", :friends [-2]}, {:db/id -2, :name "Bob"}]

Flattening: The API worker uses its local, pre-fetched block to assign permanent IDs. It maps -1 to 1,000,000 and -2 to 1,000,001. It flattens the maps into tuples and resolves all references internally.

The Handoff: The API worker serializes the fully resolved, flat datoms and sends them to the Transactor's queue.

The Response: The API worker immediately responds to the user with the translation map ({-1: 1000000, -2: 1000001}), while the Transactor blindly and blazingly fast appends the data to the MemTables and the WAL.

If an API worker crashes, we might "lose" a few thousand unused IDs from its block. In a u64 space, a few thousand skipped numbers are mathematically irrelevant. We will run out of atoms in the universe before we run out of u64 IDs.

The Next Massive Performance Hurdle: Updates and Retractions
Now that we have data flying into the Transactor perfectly formatted, we have to make a crucial decision about how we handle state changes in our bitemporal model.

Let's say Entity 123 has the name "Alice". Today, we want to update her name to "Alice Smith".

Because we are bitemporal, we track Valid_From and Valid_To.

Option A: Read-Before-Write (The Clean Storage Approach)
When the Transactor receives the new name "Alice Smith", it pauses, scans the recent MemTables (or even disk) to find the old "Alice" datom, and updates its Valid_To timestamp to Now. Then it appends the new "Alice Smith" datom.

Tradeoff: Storage is perfectly clean and ready to be queried instantly, but writes are penalized heavily because the Transactor must perform a read/search before every update.

Option B: Append-Only Retractions (The Fast Ingestion Approach)
The Transactor does absolutely no reading. When the API worker submits the update, it submits two datoms simultaneously: a retraction for the old state and an assertion for the new state:

[123, :name, "Alice", Op=False, Valid_From=Now]

[123, :name, "Alice Smith", Op=True, Valid_From=Now]
The Transactor just blindly appends both.

Tradeoff: Writes are incredibly fast (100% append-only). However, the burden shifts to the Query Nodes and the background Compaction threads. When a Query Node reads the data, it has to dynamically match the retractions (Op=False) to the original assertions (Op=True) to figure out the Valid_To boundaries on the fly.

Given our absolute dedication to maximum write throughput and indefinite read scalability, Option B (Append-Only) is the classic columnar approach, but it makes the DataFusion query logic more complex.

Are you comfortable pushing this temporal resolution complexity onto the read-side (and background compaction threads) to keep our ingestion speed at its absolute theoretical maximum?

Let's explore some rough percentages in performance penalties on both the read and write side of things. What percentage of write throughput do we lose in Option A, and what percentage in Option B? Similarly, what percentage do we lose in reads for Options A and B? Additionally, just because we lose a certain percentage, what would our overall throughput be, per second for both options? I know it's hard to determine what read throughput would be as it heavily depends on how complex the query is but come up with a semi-hypothetical number of milliseconds or seconds for a moderately complex query of a depth of 5 joins for each approach so we can discuss read throughput in a more tangible light, as opposed to purely theoretical and/or percentages. However, also produce the best guess percentages you can, as well.

This is exactly how database engineers have to think. Theoretical elegance means nothing if the system chokes under load. To put tangible numbers on this, let's establish a baseline: we are running on a modern server (e.g., 16 cores, NVMe SSD) and our single-threaded Transactor is optimized purely for log-appending in memory.

Our baseline theoretical maximum for pure append-only ingestion in a tight Rust loop is roughly 500,000 datoms per second per core.

Here is the brutal reality of how Options A and B impact that baseline.

Option A: Read-Before-Write (The Clean Storage Approach)
In this approach, to maintain perfectly clean temporal boundaries, the Transactor must find the prior state of the entity/attribute, mutate its Valid_To timestamp, and then insert the new state.

Write Throughput Penalty: ~80% to 90% loss.

Why? You are destroying your CPU cache. Instead of a tight sequential memory copy, the Transactor must halt, execute a B-Tree or Hash Map lookup, potentially cause a cache miss, and mutate existing memory. If the prior state has already been flushed to a Parquet file on disk, you suffer a catastrophic latency spike.

Raw Write Speed: Drops from 500,000 ops/sec down to roughly 50,000 to 100,000 datoms per second.

Read Throughput Penalty: 0% (Baseline).

Why? The data is already perfectly formatted for the query engine. Valid_To boundaries are explicit.

Hypothetical Read Latency (5 Joins): ~10 milliseconds.
Because the data is clean, DataFusion just executes 5 highly optimized hash-joins across Arrow arrays.

Option B: Append-Only Retractions (The Fast Ingestion Approach)
In this approach, the Transactor blindly appends an Op=False (retraction) and an Op=True (assertion) to the end of the array.

Write Throughput Penalty: 0%.

Why? The CPU L1/L2 caches stay piping hot. We are just sequentially writing into pre-allocated memory buffers and blasting it to the WAL.

Raw Write Speed: Peak performance. ~500,000 datoms per second.

Read Throughput Penalty: ~30% to 50% loss (on uncompacted data).

Why? When the query engine reads the MemTable, it has to do extra work. Before it can execute your 5 joins, it must perform an internal "anti-join" or aggregation to match the Op=False retractions against the Op=True assertions and dynamically calculate the Valid_To boundaries in memory.

Hypothetical Read Latency (5 Joins): ~15 to 20 milliseconds. The engine spends 5-10ms resolving the bitemporal state, and then 10ms doing the actual 5 joins.

The "Cheat Code": Background Compaction
Looking at those numbers, Option B seems like we are permanently taxing our readers to subsidize our writers. But database engineering has a cheat code: Background Compaction.

The ~30% read penalty in Option B only applies to data currently sitting in the MemTable or in freshly flushed, uncompacted Parquet files. While the Transactor is blazing along at 500,000 ops/sec, we have background Rust threads completely detached from the write path. These threads quietly read the small, messy Parquet files (full of retractions), resolve the bitemporal boundaries (turning them into clean Option A-style data), and write out large, highly compressed, perfectly clean Parquet files.

The Resulting Reality of Option B:

You get the 500,000 ops/sec write speed forever.

Queries touching the last few minutes of "messy" data take ~15ms.

Queries touching historical, compacted data take the optimal ~10ms.

This is why almost every modern, high-performance analytical and columnar database (ClickHouse, DuckDB, RocksDB) uses Option B (Append-Only LSM trees). You simply cannot sacrifice the ingestion pipeline; you can always throw more cores at background compaction and read queries.

Given that Option B paired with aggressive background compaction gives us peak ingestion while maintaining high-speed reads on historical data, are you comfortable moving forward with this Append-Only architecture, or does the slight latency bump on querying "hot" (recent) data pose a problem for your specific use cases?

No, I like the "cheat code" approach of having background threads playing a bit of "cleanup" from time-to-time (especially if that frequency is measured in double- or ideally single-digit second intervals). I'm willing to sacrifice some temporary performance degradation on reads, as long as "temporary" is a short period of time. Is it true then that one core/thread would be the ingestion of highly optimized datoms and a series of other cores would be running in the background to constantly "clean up" the data on disk? Also, how does this work from a read perspective after ingestion? Is the data immediately available in memory while the write is happening or does the user have to wait for it to be persisted before it is accessible. If the data is immediately available, in memory, then I think the background approach to compacting the files makes absolute sense. Even if not, I still think it's the best of both worlds. What is not acceptable is an 80-90% degradation in write throughput or a constant, forever 30% in read throughput. It seems like we really don't have another option here, but please clarify the other questions posted here.

You have absolutely nailed the architecture. This is exactly how the world’s fastest databases—from ClickHouse to RocksDB—handle the fundamental physics of storage. You are making the right tradeoffs to ensure this database is a beast in production.

Let's clear up exactly how the cores are allocated and how the read visibility works, because this is where the design truly shines.

1. The Core Allocation (Who Does What)
Yes, your understanding is perfectly accurate. We are explicitly separating the "dumb, fast" work from the "smart, heavy" work.

The Transactor (1 Dedicated Core): This single thread has one job: take incoming arrays of data, validate the types, append them to the Arrow MemTable arrays in RAM, and stream the raw bytes to the WAL on disk. Because it does zero sorting and zero historical lookups, a single modern CPU core can easily saturate your disk's I/O bandwidth.

The Compactors (N Background Cores): We will use a thread pool (like Rust’s tokio or rayon). When the MemTable hits a size threshold (e.g., 64MB) or a time threshold (e.g., 5 seconds), it is flushed to disk as a small "Level 0" Parquet file. Immediately, the background cores wake up. They read these small files, pair up the retractions (Op=False) with the old assertions, calculate the Valid_To boundaries, sort the data, and write out large, clean "Level 1" Parquet files.

Because the Compactors run on separate threads, they never block the Transactor. You can configure them to run aggressively—every few seconds—so the "messy" data window is incredibly small.

2. Read Availability (The "Unified View")
The data is immediately available in memory. Your users do not have to wait for the background threads to compact the data, nor do they even have to wait for the data to be flushed to Parquet.

Here is exactly how a read works millisecond-by-millisecond:

The Write: A user updates a record. The Transactor appends it to the in-memory MemTable and the WAL. The moment the WAL confirms the write to disk, the Transactor returns a success response to the user.

The Query: The user immediately fires a Datalog query for that data.

The Unified Scan: The Query Node executing the Datalog query does a "scatter-gather" across two distinct storage tiers:

It scans the large, clean Parquet files on disk (where the heavy 5-join operations run at maximum speed).

Simultaneously, it scans the hot MemTable sitting directly in RAM.

The Merge: The query engine merges the results on the fly. It applies the "messy" retractions from the in-memory MemTable over the clean data from disk.

Because the MemTable is entirely in RAM and relatively small (representing only the last few seconds of writes), scanning it and resolving its bitemporal overlaps is virtually instantaneous. The 30% performance penalty we discussed only applies to the engine processing this tiny, in-memory slice of data, not the millions of historical records on disk.

The Best of Both Worlds
You achieve the holy grail:

Writes are instantaneous (appended to memory and WAL).

Reads are strongly consistent (you immediately query the in-memory data).

Historical queries are blazingly fast (the background cores ensure the bulk of your data is perfectly clean and vectorized on disk).

To make this background compaction and querying as fast as possible, we need to decide how we sort our data inside the Parquet files. Datomic famously maintains four covering indexes (sorted trees): EAVT (Entity lookups), AEVT (Column/Attribute scans), AVET (Value lookups/Filters), and VAET (Reverse graph traversals).

Since sorting and writing four identical copies of the data will increase our background compaction time and storage footprint, should we build our engine to maintain all four indexes right out of the gate, or start with just a primary pair (like EAVT and AEVT) to optimize our initial build?

Let's explore indexing as it was something I wanted to get to but needed the other components to be understood first. I think having all indexes makes the most sense for query performance. Given that the on-disk format is Parquet and the in-memory format is Apache Arrow, what kinda of disk space are we looking at by having every permutation stored for fast retrieval and what performance penalty are we paying for compacting all these indexes? Also, do we need an index for each thing like Valid_From/Valid_To, in addition to the variations of E-A-V-T?

You are hitting on the exact crux of database performance: the storage-versus-compute tradeoff. Storing every permutation (EAVT, AEVT, AVET, VAET) is the Datomic way, and it is precisely why its read queries are so blazingly fast. It guarantees that almost any Datalog query can be resolved through a contiguous, sequential scan.

Let’s break down the physical realities of adding all four covering indexes to our Rust/Parquet architecture, and how bitemporality fits into the sorting.

1. The Disk Space Reality (The Parquet Compression Magic)
If you logically duplicate your data four times, you might expect a 400% increase in disk space. In a row-based database (like PostgreSQL), this is exactly what happens. But in a columnar format like Apache Parquet, the math is entirely different.

Parquet compresses data per column using algorithms like Run-Length Encoding (RLE) and Dictionary Encoding.

AEVT Index Example: In an AEVT-sorted Parquet file, the Attribute (A) column is grouped together. If you have 10,000 datoms for the :user/age attribute, the A column looks like [:user/age, :user/age, :user/age...] 10,000 times. Parquet's RLE simply stores [Value: :user/age, Count: 10,000]. That entire column shrinks to a few bytes.

The Reality: Because sorting drastically increases consecutive identical values in columnar data, compression ratios skyrocket. A 4x logical duplication in Parquet usually results in only a 1.5x to 2.5x increase in actual disk footprint compared to storing a single unindexed, uncompressed dataset.

Given how cheap NVMe storage is today, paying a 2x storage penalty to achieve O(1) query planning and sequential read speeds is the best trade you can make.

2. The Compaction Performance Penalty (Write Amplification)
While storage is cheap, CPU and Disk I/O bandwidth are not. Generating all four indexes means that when your background Compactors wake up to process a 64MB MemTable flush, they must:

Sort the data four distinct ways in RAM.

Compress the data four distinct ways.

Write four distinct Parquet files to disk.

The Penalty: You are paying a 4x Write Amplification penalty during background compaction. If your ingestion rate is extremely high, your background threads might saturate the disk's write bandwidth trying to write out all four indexes, causing the compaction queue to back up.

The Solution: We don't block the Transactor, but we do need robust thread-pool sizing. We can easily afford this penalty on a multi-core machine because sorting Arrow arrays using Rust's slice::sort_unstable_by (or DataFusion's highly optimized sort kernels) is incredibly fast. We just dedicate 4-8 background cores purely to index generation.

3. Do We Need Indexes for Valid_From / Valid_To?
No, we do not need distinct, time-first indexes (like ValidFrom_E_A_V). This is where Parquet's metadata features save us from combinatorial explosion.

Instead of making time the primary sort key, we append our bitemporal fields to the end of our core indexes. Our physical sorting looks like this:

EAVT: (Entity, Attribute, Value, Valid_From, Valid_To, System_T)

AEVT: (Attribute, Entity, Value, Valid_From, Valid_To, System_T)

AVET: (Attribute, Value, Entity, Valid_From, Valid_To, System_T)

VAET: (Value, Attribute, Entity, Valid_From, Valid_To, System_T)

How queries use this without a time index:
Parquet files are broken into "Row Groups" (e.g., chunks of 1 million rows). For every single column in a Row Group, Parquet stores Min/Max Statistics (also known as Zone Maps) in the file footer.

If a user executes a query looking for Alice’s :user/age as it was in the year 2023, the query engine (DataFusion) does the following:

It looks for the AEVT Parquet files.

It quickly binary-searches the file footers to find the Row Groups containing A = :user/age and E = Alice.

Before it actually reads the data from disk into RAM, it checks the Min/Max stats for the Valid_From column in that Row Group.

If the Max(Valid_From) in that chunk is from 2022, DataFusion completely ignores that Row Group. It reads nothing from disk.

Because Parquet's block-level metadata aggressively prunes data based on time filters, making time a suffix in our standard indexes gives us bitemporal query performance that is nearly as fast as having a dedicated time index, but without the massive storage and I/O bloat.

Given that writing all four covering indexes at once creates a 4x background I/O spike, would you prefer to generate all four immediately during the first "Level 0 to Level 1" compaction phase to guarantee instant query performance, or should we dynamically defer the heavier indexes (like AVET and VAET) to a slower, secondary background process so we don't overwhelm the disk during burst ingestions?

The pattern here will be to continually explore the tradeoffs in performance. At this point, we've introduced a new tradeoff: write amplification and disk I/O throughput. We need to be careful about not requiring a huge, expensive infrastructure just to run the database - something Datomic is not particularly known for. However, it's reasonable to design the system in a way that to get maximum throughput, it requires at least 4 CPU cores and 16GB of RAM. If this is not possible, or dramatically changes the overall architecture, let's look at pros and cons to make decisions. However, if we can design the database to scale with resources, that would be ideal. Let's also look at the tradeoff of memory for queries versus the ideal rate at which to compact the indexes for the purpose of finding a balance of overall throughput compared to user perspective of experience. That is: can we keep the background compactors busy but not putting to much strain on the system that it causes a noticeable degradation in both read and write performance. Let's explore our options.

This is the exact right mindset. Designing a system that demands a 64-core cluster just to idle is an anti-pattern. A baseline of 4 cores and 16GB of RAM is a fantastic, realistic target for a "small production" or "heavy local development" node, and it forces us to be disciplined about our resource economics.

To make this database scale gracefully from a 4-core laptop up to a 64-core AWS metal instance without changing the architecture, we have to introduce Staged Indexing (Tiered Compaction) and Adaptive Memory Thresholds.

Here is how we balance the write amplification, memory footprint, and user experience within those resource constraints.

1. The 4-Core / 16GB RAM Resource Map
If we have exactly 4 cores, we must strictly partition our threads so they don't fight for L1/L2 cache or lock up the OS:

Core 1: The Transactor. 100% dedicated to the single-writer ingestion loop and WAL.

Core 2: The Query Engine. Dedicated to serving user reads (DataFusion execution).

Core 3 & 4: The Compactors. The background workers managing Parquet files and indexes.

RAM (16GB): We dedicate ~4GB to the active MemTable (and its RCU snapshots for queries), ~8GB to DataFusion/Arrow for caching Parquet footers and executing joins, and leave 4GB for the OS and page cache.

2. Solving Write Amplification: Staged Index Generation
If Cores 3 and 4 try to generate EAVT, AEVT, AVET, and VAET all at once every time a MemTable flushes, they will peg the CPU at 100% and saturate the disk I/O, causing the OS to choke on Core 1 and 2's operations.

Instead, we borrow from classic LSM-tree design and stagger the work based on query necessity:

Level 0 (The Flush): When the MemTable fills up, Cores 3/4 write a single Parquet file sorted by Time and Entity (T, E). This is extremely fast because the data is naturally ordered by Time arriving at the Transactor. Disk I/O impact: Minimal.

Level 1 (The Primary Indexes): When Level 0 files accumulate, Cores 3/4 merge them into EAVT and AEVT Parquet files. These are the two most critical indexes for 90% of Datalog queries (Entity lookups and Attribute scans).

Level 2 (The Heavy Indexes): Only when the system detects low I/O pressure (or on a delayed schedule), it reads the Level 1 files and generates the AVET (Value search/Filtering) and VAET (Reverse graph traversal) indexes.

The User Experience Impact: If a user runs a reverse-graph query (VAET) immediately after inserting data, DataFusion still answers it correctly. It just has to use the in-memory MemTable or do a slightly slower hash-filter on the EAVT files until the true VAET index is built a few minutes later.

3. Memory vs. Compaction Frequency (The Tuning Dial)
We need to balance how long data stays in memory (which slows down reads) versus how often we flush to disk (which increases write amplification).

The "Micro-Batch" Problem: If we flush every 1 second (e.g., 10MB chunks), memory usage is tiny, but Cores 3 and 4 will be constantly creating thousands of tiny Parquet files. DataFusion will struggle to read 1,000 tiny files, and disk metadata overhead will skyrocket.

The "Mega-Batch" Problem: If we flush every 5 minutes (e.g., 2GB chunks), disk I/O is highly optimized. But the MemTable grows massive. Every time Core 2 executes a read query, it has to scan a 2GB unindexed memory buffer and resolve millions of bitemporal overlaps on the fly. Read latency will spike noticeably.

The Solution: Adaptive Backpressure
We don't use a hardcoded timer. We use memory and I/O thresholds.

The MemTable flushes only when it hits a "Goldilocks" size for Parquet Row Groups (e.g., 64MB to 128MB). On a busy system, this might be every 5 seconds. On a quiet system, it might be every 2 hours.

If the Transactor is ingesting data so violently that the MemTable hits 500MB before Cores 3/4 can finish their Level 0 flush, the database initiates Backpressure. The Transactor artificially slows down its acknowledgment to API workers (e.g., adding a 5ms delay) to let the background I/O catch its breath.

This guarantees the database never runs out of memory and never completely locks up, smoothly degrading write throughput only when the physical limits of the SSD are reached.

By staggering the index generation and tuning the MemTable size to Parquet's sweet spot (~64MB), a 4-core/16GB machine can comfortably handle thousands of inserts per second while maintaining highly responsive Datalog queries.

As we think about that Level 2 index generation, how critical are heavy reverse-graph traversals (VAET) and full-text value lookups (AVET) for your expected day-one workloads—do they need to be instantly optimized, or is it acceptable if those specific access patterns take a few seconds longer on newly ingested data while the background cores catch up?

Let's define the term "few seconds longer on newly ingested data". Are those "few seconds" affecting the read performance for let's say a full-text search? Are they affecting the general query response time by increasing it in seconds? What exactly is being affected and by how much?

I need to correct my previous phrasing because it was dangerously ambiguous. When I said "takes a few seconds longer," I meant the window of time that the data exists in an unindexed state, not the latency added to your query response time.

If your database added seconds of latency to a query, it would be broken. Let's look at exactly what is affected and the true mathematical cost in milliseconds.

The True Cost of Missing Level 2 Indexes
Let’s say the background compactors haven't built the AVET (Value) or VAET (Reverse Graph) indexes for the last 64MB chunk of ingested data.

What is affected?
Only queries that start their traversal by looking up a specific value or traversing backward.

AVET Example: [:find ?e :where [?e :user/email "alice@example.com"]] (Looking up an entity by an exact value).

VAET Example: [:find ?parent :where [?parent :org/employees 123]] (Finding who points to Entity 123).

How much is the query delayed?

With the Index (Ideal): DataFusion does a binary search on the Parquet footer, finds the exact row, and returns it. Latency: ~1 to 5 milliseconds.

Without the Index (The Penalty): DataFusion cannot do a binary search. Instead, it must do a "Table Scan" across the Value column of that specific 64MB unindexed chunk.

The Math: A 64MB chunk is roughly 1 to 2 million datoms. Because DataFusion uses Apache Arrow, it scans columns using CPU SIMD (Single Instruction, Multiple Data) instructions. Scanning 2 million strings in RAM to find "alice@example.com" takes a modern CPU roughly 10 to 25 milliseconds.

The Conclusion: The performance penalty for querying unindexed "hot" data is about 10 to 25 milliseconds, not seconds. For 99% of web applications, this degradation is entirely imperceptible to the end user.

A Critical Distinction: Full-Text Search vs. Value Lookup
You mentioned "full-text search," which brings up a vital architectural point.

The AVET index is strictly for exact value matching (e.g., WHERE email = 'bob@bob.com'). It is not a Full-Text Search (FTS) engine. If you want to do fuzzy matching, tokenization, or relevance scoring (e.g., searching for "quick brown fox" inside a 5,000-word blog post string), an AVET index will not help you.

If true Full-Text Search is a requirement for this database, we cannot rely on Parquet/Arrow sorting. We would need to embed a dedicated Rust search library like Tantivy (the Rust equivalent of Apache Lucene/Elasticsearch).

How it would work: The Transactor would pass string datoms to the Tantivy thread. Tantivy builds an inverted index on disk. When a user writes a Datalog query like [:find ?e :where [(fulltext $ :post/body "fox") [[?e ?score]]]], DataFusion reaches out to Tantivy, gets the matching Entity IDs, and joins them back into the main query.

Given this clarification on latency (milliseconds, not seconds) and the distinction regarding search, do you want this database to support true, Lucene-style Full-Text Search via something like Tantivy, or are exact-match and prefix-match value lookups (AVET) sufficient for the data model you are envisioning?

One of the things I like about Datalevin (has native FTS) and XTDB 1.x (integrates Apache Lucene) is the ability to have full-text search. I think I would like this database (which we're going to name "MesoDB" for a working title to be able to talk about it) to have an optional full-text search engine that is native to how it works internally - not ETL'ing data out to Elasticsearch or another DB just for search capabilities. Let's go ahead and integrate this but make it something that can be turned on/off depending on the user's preferred use case. While we're on the topic of options: let's make it possible to set the number of threads and min/max memory, CPU, GPU, etc in a configuration file (let's call it mesodb.toml as we'll use TOML for the format) where the user can tune their system and enable/disable features based on what they need out of the system and how many resources (CPU, RAM) they have to give it.

I love the name MesoDB. It has a solid, foundational ring to it, perfectly fitting for a core data infrastructure project.

Integrating a native, toggleable Full-Text Search (FTS) engine without relying on an external ETL pipeline is a massive win for developer experience. Datalevin and XTDB got this exactly right. In the Rust ecosystem, we have the perfect tool for this: Tantivy. It is a blazing-fast search engine library heavily inspired by Apache Lucene, but written in pure Rust.

Let's break down how we integrate Tantivy natively into MesoDB's architecture, and then design the mesodb.toml to give users absolute control over their hardware.

1. Native FTS via Tantivy (The Architecture)
Because Tantivy builds an inverted index (mapping words to the Entity IDs that contain them) rather than a columnar index, we keep its storage separate from our Parquet files but tightly coupled in our execution engine.

Ingestion (The Write Path): We do not want FTS indexing to slow down our ultra-fast Transactor. When the Transactor processes a transaction, it checks if any string attributes are flagged for full-text search. If they are, it asynchronously pushes a copy of that (Entity, Attribute, Value) tuple into a fast, lock-free queue. A dedicated background Tantivy thread pulls from this queue, tokenizes the text, and updates the inverted index on disk.

Querying (The Read Path): When a user writes a Datalog query like [:find ?e ?score :where [(fulltext $ :post/body "MesoDB rules") [[?e ?score]]]], our query planner intercepts the fulltext call. It asks Tantivy to execute the search, which returns an array of matching Entity IDs and their TF-IDF/BM25 scores. We instantly convert this into an Apache Arrow RecordBatch and hand it to DataFusion to InnerJoin against the rest of the bitemporal graph data.

The user gets a seamless Datalog experience, and we maintain our separation of storage and compute.

2. Tuning the Engine: mesodb.toml
Exposing the underlying knobs of Tokio (Rust's async runtime), DataFusion, and Tantivy allows MesoDB to scale from a Raspberry Pi up to a massive AWS metal instance.

Here is what our mesodb.toml structure would look like:

Ini, TOML
# mesodb.toml

[system]
# The directory where the WAL, Parquet files, and Tantivy indexes live.
data_dir = "/var/lib/mesodb/data"

[compute]
# Number of threads dedicated to API requests and query planning.
# If 0, MesoDB automatically uses the number of physical CPU cores.
api_threads = 0
# Number of background threads for Parquet compaction and sorting.
compaction_threads = 4

[memory]
# The maximum size the in-memory Arrow MemTable can reach before flushing to Level 0 Parquet.
memtable_flush_threshold_mb = 64
# Maximum RAM allocated for DataFusion query execution (joins, aggregations).
# Prevents OOM crashes on massive queries.
query_execution_limit_gb = 8

[search]
# Toggle the native Tantivy FTS engine.
enabled = true
# How much RAM Tantivy can use for building the inverted index before flushing to disk.
indexer_memory_mb = 128

[advanced]
# GPU Acceleration (Experimental)
# DataFusion can be extended with cuDF/CUDA to process Arrow arrays on GPUs.
enable_gpu_vectorization = false
3. How the Configuration Maps to Rust
When MesoDB boots up, we read this TOML file and use it to configure our underlying libraries:

compute.api_threads: We pass this directly to the tokio::runtime::Builder to restrict or expand the core async worker pool.

memory.query_execution_limit_gb: DataFusion has a built-in MemoryPool trait. We configure this to aggressively spill intermediate join/sort data to disk if a rogue query tries to consume more than the allocated 8GB, keeping the database stable.

search.enabled: If false, the Tantivy indexer thread is never spawned, the FTS queues are dropped, and any Datalog query attempting to use (fulltext ...) returns an immediate schema error. Zero overhead for users who don't need it.

Adding GPU support to the config is a great forward-thinking move. While building the CPU-bound DataFusion execution is our primary goal, the Arrow ecosystem is rapidly building out GPU processing (like NVIDIA's RAPIDS). Having the toggle in the config means we can cleanly isolate that feature behind Rust conditional compilation (#[cfg(feature = "gpu")]) later.

With the FTS engine defined and our hardware resource allocation configurable via TOML, we need to think about how MesoDB knows what to put into that Tantivy index.

How should a user tell MesoDB which attributes should be full-text indexed—should it be declared via a global configuration file, or should it be an explicit, bitemporal property defined on the Attribute's schema datom itself (e.g., asserting [:db/add :post/body :db/fulltext true])?

I don't have an immediate, concrete answer to that but I do like the idea of it being configurable for both performance and resource use.

It is completely fine to keep that flexible right now. In fact, combining both concepts—system-level configuration and schema-level precision—is exactly how we achieve the ultimate control over performance and resources.

Here is how we can harmonize the two approaches for MesoDB:

The Hardware vs. Data Boundary
We can split the responsibility of full-text search (FTS) configuration to give the user absolute control:

The TOML Defines the Hardware Boundaries: As we outlined in mesodb.toml, the configuration file dictates if the Tantivy engine boots up at all, and exactly how much RAM and CPU it is allowed to consume. If a user is on a constrained device, they can throttle the indexer or disable it entirely, regardless of what the data looks like.

The Schema Defines the Data Boundaries: We adopt the elegant Datomic approach for the data itself. A user explicitly flags an attribute for FTS using a system datom: [:db/add :post/body :db/fulltext true].

Why this is the perfect performance safeguard:
If MesoDB automatically tried to full-text index every string value, it would waste massive amounts of disk space and CPU cycles indexing things like base64 image strings, session tokens, or encrypted password hashes. By requiring the user to explicitly flag :db/fulltext true on specific attributes, we ensure the Tantivy background thread only spends its precious RAM and CPU on actual searchable text.

This gives us the bitemporal elegance of a Datalog schema with the hard physical boundaries of a systems-level TOML file.

Reviewing the Architecture
We have just successfully architected the high-level design of a profoundly powerful database. Let's recap what MesoDB is fundamentally built on:

In-Memory Engine: Apache Arrow (strongly-typed, columnar, SIMD-accelerated).

On-Disk Persistence: Apache Parquet (highly compressed, dictionary-encoded, Min/Max block stats).

Durability: A zero-copy rkyv Write-Ahead Log (WAL) with group-commit for ultra-fast, single-threaded ingestion.

Query Execution: Apache DataFusion (executing our custom Datalog AST as vectorized relational algebra).

Full-Text Search: Native, toggleable integration with Tantivy (Rust's Lucene equivalent).

Concurrency Model: A single-threaded Transactor for write perfection, paired with background thread pools for compaction and infinite lock-free read scalability.

This is a phenomenal, production-grade foundation.

To take our next step toward actual Rust implementation, we need to decide how users will interact with MesoDB. Should we build MesoDB first as an embedded Rust library (similar to SQLite, RocksDB, or the in-memory Datascript), or should we immediately start designing it as a standalone server with a network boundary (like gRPC or an HTTP API)?

Not to throw too many curveballs at you, but... let's do both! Let's implement it so that it can be embedded like SQLite (with eventual bindings for Rust, C/C++, Java, Python, Ruby, etc) and have a very thin, high-performance wire protocol for running it as a single- or clustered-architecture. Giving the user options is what makes things like XTDB 1.x great, and where in my opinion, they missed a great opportunity/feature when they made XTDB 2.x server-only. To start, however, we can implement the "core" of our database as a standalone binary that we can then build on top of to add a network/wire protocol layer to later. If there's a better way to architect this, please make a suggestion but the gist is that we will have both: a standalone binary/embeddable database and a full-blown server infrastructure for production use cases requiring replication, redundancy and failover.

That is not a curveball at all; in fact, it is the absolute best practice for building complex systems in Rust. You are spot-on about XTDB 1.x—giving developers the ability to run an in-memory or embedded database for testing/small apps, and then scale that exact same engine out to a distributed cluster, is a massive competitive advantage.

In Rust, the way we architect this is using a Cargo Workspace. We do not build a "standalone binary" that we later try to hack into a library. Instead, we build the engine strictly as a library crate first, and the server binary is just a very thin wrapper that imports that library.

Here is exactly how we structure the MesoDB repository to achieve this dual-mode superpower.

The MesoDB Cargo Workspace
We will split the codebase into distinct Rust crates within a single workspace:

1. mesodb-core (The Embedded Library)
This is the heart of the database. It contains the Transactor, the WAL, DataFusion, Tantivy, and the Parquet flushing logic.

Rule: There is absolutely zero network code in this crate. No HTTP, no gRPC, no sockets.

The API: It exposes a clean, programmatic Rust API.

FFI (Foreign Function Interface): Because this is pure Rust, we can eventually use tools like PyO3 (for Python), JNI (for Java), or standard C-bindings (cbindgen) to wrap this exact core so developers can embed MesoDB directly into their Python scripts or Node.js backends, exactly like SQLite.

2. mesodb-server (The Network Node)
This is our standalone binary for production clusters.

The Wrapper: This crate just imports mesodb-core and spins it up.

The Network: It runs a high-performance network stack (e.g., Tokio + Axum for HTTP, or Tonic for gRPC) to accept remote connections.

Clustering/Replication: Later, when we add High Availability (HA), we implement a consensus algorithm like Raft (using Rust crates like openraft) here in the server crate. The replication log coordinates with other nodes and then feeds deterministic transactions down into the local mesodb-core. The core doesn't even need to know it is part of a cluster.

3. mesodb-cli (The Terminal Client)
A lightweight command-line tool (built with clap) that connects to mesodb-server or instantiates mesodb-core directly for local debugging and REPL interactions.

The Wire Protocol: Apache Arrow Flight
You mentioned wanting a "very thin, high-performance wire protocol." Since our entire in-memory representation is Apache Arrow, we get to use an incredible piece of technology: Arrow Flight.

Arrow Flight is an RPC framework (built on top of gRPC) specifically designed for streaming columnar Arrow data over the network.

Standard gRPC/JSON: You query the database -> the DB serializes the data to JSON/Protobuf -> sends it over the wire -> the client deserializes it back into memory. (Huge CPU penalty).

Arrow Flight: You query MesoDB -> DataFusion produces an Arrow RecordBatch in RAM -> Flight sends the exact binary layout over the TCP socket -> the client receives it and drops the pointer into memory. Zero-copy, zero-deserialization over the network.

If a Python client queries the mesodb-server over Arrow Flight, the data lands directly into a Pandas or Polars dataframe instantaneously.

Putting It All Together
By designing mesodb-core as an agnostic storage and compute engine, you get your SQLite-style embedded experience for free. If a Rust developer wants to build a desktop app, they just add mesodb-core = "0.1" to their Cargo.toml, and the Transactor and Compactors run quietly in the background threads of their app. If a DevOps team wants a highly available cluster, they deploy the mesodb-server binary using Docker/Kubernetes.

This architecture gives us a clear roadmap to start coding. To kick off the mesodb-core embedded API, should we expose a purely synchronous interface (e.g., db.transact(...)) to make it exceptionally easy for developers to embed, or should we force an entirely asynchronous API (db.transact(...).await) given that our underlying DataFusion and Tokio components will rely heavily on async execution?

Let's explore sync vs async. I think it's possible to have both, starting with the sync API. Does this add considerable complexity to mesodb-core to have both a sync API and async API?

Providing both a synchronous and asynchronous API is the hallmark of a premium, developer-friendly Rust library (the HTTP client reqwest is a famous example of doing this perfectly).

To directly answer your question: It does add complexity, but the complexity is mostly in maintaining the public API surface, not in the core database logic. However, there is a massive "plot twist" regarding your suggestion to start with the sync API. In our specific stack, starting with a purely synchronous internal implementation is actually a trap.

Here is the technical reality of why we must build the engine asynchronously, and how we can easily expose both APIs to the user.

The Impedance Mismatch
The core technologies we have chosen—Apache DataFusion (for queries) and Tokio (for our background compaction and WAL threads)—are deeply, fundamentally asynchronous.

DataFusion uses async rust to read Parquet files concurrently, stream Arrow batches, and execute query plans across multiple cores.
